*R.3.2(1)PART 3:COURT ACTIONS suit commenced without leave given during a federally-legislated moratorium is a nullity. So is a statutory suit (even a proposed class action begun) without the leave to sue which legislation imposes.

Once litigation has got as far as the Supreme Court of Canada and is at an end, it is proper for the trial court to order that no new documents be filed by the plaintiff. That is ordered from time to time with persistent or vexatious litigants. An existing consent suit by one person for a narrow purpose should not be used by the opposing party for very different new complaints, albeit between the same parties about the same

company, but that does not remove the court's jurisdiction to give the new claimant relief. A consent order is enough consent to an Applications Judge hearing a matter like an appeal. An Applications Judge cannot givecertiorari, but can give a declaration. When a suit, even for divorce, is commenced in one judicial centre, any motion in it should

be brought in the same judicial centre, especially if there are lawyers in that judicial centre experienced in that type of litigation. *How to Start an Action*3.2(1) An action may be started only by filing in the appropriate judicial centre*determined under rule 3.3 [Determining the appropriate judicial centre]

*(a)there is no substantial factual dispute,*(b)there is no person to serve as defendant,*(c)a decision, act or omission of a person or body is to be the subject*of judicial review,*(d)an enactment authorizes or requires an application,an originating*application,an originating notice, a notice of motion or a petition to

12Badawy v.Hamad2019 ABQB 786, JCC 4801 182511 (Oct 8). *R.3.2(6)PART 3:COURT ACTIONS
*A.General*1.Effect of Errors in Commencement This Rule combines a number of former Rules dealing with the manner of commencing an action. Any party alleging that a proceeding is a nullity because it was not filed properly (the

respondent) has the onus of proof, because of the presumption of regularity.Though the court where a proceeding should have been filed had no copy of the notice on its file, it was likely that the notice was given to staff of the correct court, and that any omission was theirs, not the appellant's. Commencing a suit the wrong way is usually not fatal, for 3 reasons.First, R.3.2(6) says

so. Second, any suit can be started by statement of claim, and an Originating Application is never compulsory, and even legislation is not lightly read as saying the opposite. When a statute permits an Originating Application in a certain situation, that is not mandatory, and a statement of claim instead is permissible. Third, R.3.12 lets the court turn an Originating Application into a full-

fledged suit. Otherwise where the court can give the relief, it should be continued as a statement of claim under R.3.2(4). However, where no viable cause of action is pled in a statement of claim and no ground for judicial review (like bias) is alleged, and quashing is not sought, then the court

cannot convert the statement of claim into a judicial review application. If the Court of King's Bench is the right court to hear a matter, the wrong procedure (judicial review vs.suit for declaration) is not a fatal flaw, especially when much time has elapsed and the standard of review is the same with either procedure.

Where a suit is brought by a company struck off the Register, legislation protecting "rights acquired by any person prior to the revival", on the revival of a company to the register, does not refer to a potential defence of lack of parties. So revival usually cures the defect.Denying a plaintiff company's incorporation sufficiently pleads its lack of legal personality, and a company struck off cannot start a suit, and the possibility of cure by restoration is irrelevant if it has not

been restored. On a statement of claim issued despite a vexatious-litigant order forbidding suits without leave, see theBadawycase. 1For the general philosophy and approach, see the annotation to R.1.5 above.

The Clerk should not refuse to issue a statement of claim for defects in form. And the court may have the power to decide when a commencement document actually received is"filed" and change the Clerk's view of when.

*2.Examples It did not matter that an application was commenced as an appeal, rather than as judicial review;the proceeding could be converted into the proper form as long as there was no prejudice. On the form of service differing depending on whether the originating application is for judicial review or for an appeal, see thePiercecase.

An ordinary application (formerly notice of motion) is no kind of commencement document; even in a builder's lien suit? Nor does a Notice to Attend (Family Docket Court). But when the law allows a new claimant to piggyback on an existing suit (as inWhitson, re in builders'liens or foreclosures).

but all other necessary procedure was observed and there was no prejudice;it was allowed to proceed. Statutes often allow the court or a judge to give an order or a certificate for some statutory purpose.For example, to shorten the time to take proceedings on a caveat against land, or to

direct discharge of a mortgage which has been paid off.Such legislation rarely indicates what Some types of proceeding can be brought by other means than a statement of claim, even sometimes a written application (what used to be called a notice of motion), e.g.R.9.24 (to set aside a fraudulent conveyance).Even if it was wrongly commenced, that would be curable.And it can be an"action" even if commenced that way, the word"action"being broad and non-technical.

Rule 3.2(4) is generally very useful, and has a specific function not obvious at first.Sometimes a statute or the Rules prescribe a form or wording for a document.A party may depart from that wording either accidentally, or because the prescribed wording does not fit the facts at hand.Even if the form is in a statute, that is not fatal. See also the commentary on Rr.1.5 and 13.16. There aredictathat Part 30 of the former Rules of Court and their R.394 could be used to get

an injunction in a"medical emergency" case and that a statement of claim was not necessary. It is clear that an Originating Application is only to be used where a specific Rule or statute authorizes it, and where all the preconditions in that Rule or statute are met.

There is no point in deliberately starting a suit the wrong way, having one's opponent make a big fuss, and ending by having the court turn it into a full suit.That just wastes time and money. Litigants often seek wonderful shortcuts which will decide a lawsuit in 2 weeks, but such magic lamps probably do not exist, and an Originating Application is not one. Use of an Originating Application is discretionary and the court can reject it if inappropriate.

The court is likely to turn one into a full-fledged suit if * trial with live witnesses is desirable, e.g., where opposing evidence clashes, or * discovery of any kind is desirable, or * no Rule or statute authorizes an Originating Application.

If an Originating Application seeks a declaration and injunctions, and no significant facts were in issue, and no order directed different procedure, nor adopted Part 4 or Part 5, a counterclaim or affidavit of records or a trial is improper.The matter should be heard in chambers as a special application. If the plaintiff was unsure whether relief from a Commission hearing should come from judicial

review or appeal, it used to be common to seek both in the same originating document.The two proceedings do have differences in form, standards, and grounds of review.The Supreme Court of Canada now says that the standard of review of the two routes is very different.But R.3.2 allows curing an action commenced in the wrong form. There are serious criticisms of a motion by a respondent (under R.3.68) to strike out an

Originating Application seeking judicial review, or to dismiss it summarily.If the facts make the motion arguable, all the practicalities and times likely to be saved or wasted may well favor going 1For doubts as to whether one use of an Originating Application was proper, seeStay Steel & Pipe v. *Calgary (City)2001 ABCA 313.To give an injunction to enforce obedience to public health statutes a statement of claim would be necessary, not an Originating Application:Cap.Health v. *2.No Disputed Facts Subrule 3.2(2)(a) is very popular.Plaintiffs who want to avoid the delay and expense of a full lawsuit often resort to it.Formerly, the subrule could only be used where the interpretation of an enactment or instrument was involved, but that limitation has been removed.However, there is no

discretion to waive its remaining requirements, and one can use this subrule only if one satisfies each of its tests and no material facts are in dispute. Other cases approach other aspects of the question.Two steps must be followed under R. 3.2(2)(a).First three requirements must be satisfied:real question, real interest in advancing it on

one side and in opposing it on the other, and small likelihood that evidence at trial would affect the outcome.The second step is an unfettered discretion as to whether to hear the matter summarily. The Court of Appeal had directed that a suit be converted from judicial review (begun by Originating Application) to a regular suit begun by statement of claim.It said that was because of "emerging substantial factual disputes between the parties", which phrase uses the exact wording

of part of R.3.2(2)(a).Presumably the Court of Appeal thought discoveries and a conventional trial more suitable for the factual disputes.It would go against that direction to give the defendant summary trial;which would be very similar to the procedure which the Court of Appeal held inappropriate. 1C.U.P.E.v. To satisfy R.3.2(2)(a), there cannot be any relevant facts which the parties dispute, nor can there be facts which are not known and which might affect the legal answer.For example, it is impossible to decideCharterissues without facts, if only evidence under s.1 of theCharter.

A constitutional challenge is no exception to the usual Rules.It can be begun by Originating Application only if there are agreed, admitted, or assumed facts.It is dangerous to decide a An originating application is suitable if any dispute is not material or substantial, or is not

constitutional case without sufficient facts and a concrete issue. One can sometimes get a declaration that a Regulation is void. But that is not to say that R.3.2 can be used;a statement of claim may be necessary. factual.How to interpret a clause in a written contract is not a factual dispute.

If there are no factual disputes, an Originating Application may be used to decide which insurers have a duty to defend another suit. *3.Opposing an Originating Application It is not necessary to start a cross-suit or cross-motion to object to a pending application.

If there is adequate notice that the respondent is going to make an objection or ask for some variation (e.g.notice in an affidavit), that is sufficient. Especially if the relevant provision under which the originating application was brought allows the court to hear it in a summary manner. appears arguable and the Clerk is blamed for refusing to set the case down within the limitation period, the court would have to call for evidence about the clerk's refusal.

*4.Procedure and Parties To start an Originating Application, one must book a date (which will likely be over a year away).Then within a certain deadline, one must file all the necessary papers, or the application will be struck, without any chance to explain or get a waiver of any kind for any reason.The booking entry and the filing request are to different people and addresses.The filing must include

all papers that will be needed for hearing the application, and most of those must be filed in two different places, one of which has never been announced to the public or the profession, nor put into some part of the Court of King's Bench website, so far as can be discovered.(The Clerk may write to the applicant's solicitor and tell about that.) That can lead to difficulties or impossibilities in some situations, such as a.where the applicant wants to resist the application because he or she is directly

affected, but was not named as a respondent or party; b.where the booking was not under the proper style of cause;

1An Originating Application had been ongoing for years, so more parties would make complex litigation even more complicated, and increase expenses.The judge had been trying to narrow and define the *R.3.2(6)PART 3:COURT ACTIONS c.where the current party was not the one with the duty to draft the order in question and has no copy of it, nor knowledge whether it was formally entered, nor knowledge

second time to make them available for the judge who will hear the application.

is near.

So when a person wishes to respond to someone else's booked application, before the same judge at the same time, and that person needs some leave or amendment of the style of cause or addition of a party, there seems to be no way to achieve that, especially if a deadline for filing Filing requests should be addressed to the right official, as the filing work is specialized, but learning which precise one to send them to can be difficult.Sometimes each of the two or three alternate persons says that he or she is not the right one.

Problems set out below can occur.Naming conventions on electronic filing matter a lot. Different clerks do different work or handle different types of document.A poor name can lead to significant delay.At one time, if a limitation period was close, the lawyer or a trusted student or paralegal would go to the Clerk's counter, get the document filed and paid for, and come back with the document bearing a filing stamp dated within the time limit.Now that is impossible for lawyers. Filing at the counter is forbidden then;it must be done electronically.At one time, if the Clerk was

refusing a document for valid reasons, such as some information omitted, that could be quickly fixed on the spot, with a ballpoint pen.Or by seeing a Master immediately and getting anex parte fiat.None of that is possible now. There are two ways to file electronically in King's Bench.One is by the Filing Digital Service site.Whether it will give a lawyer a written acknowledgment of filing the same day is not stated. But only certain types of document can be filed on that site.For other types of document, or if one

is not fully able to use the"FDS"system, one can file electronically via email.But a Notice to the Profession stresses that such email filing will probably not be dated the day that it is received.If the clerk's office takes some days to process the document, it will NOT be backdated to the day of receipt.So a perfectly proper document sent in two days before the limitation expired, could well be shown as filed some days after the limitation period expired.(Filing often takes 2 or 3 days, in some places even 7.).

If you realize that you are nearing a deadline, you can also file a special request to ensure that the deadline will not be missed.But there are a number of ifs, ands, and buts attached to that process.This urgent procedure can only be used with email filing, NOT with the regular F.D.S. method of electronic filing.See the King's Bench website under "Announcements/New Email- filing-procedure", for an exact procedure.Note that the Clerks seem to regard 4:00 as closing time.

The rules about adding more parties to a lawsuit, and the role of new third parties, are not the same for suits by statement of claim, and for Originating Applications (Notices).Under the latter procedure, all participants are not known at the outset, and it is unclear when "pleadings"close. However, the court must see that adding needed parties is done, and that there would be no undue prejudice to the other parties.

More generally, there are textbooks on declarations.See the C.P.E., Chap.52, especially Part C.

(a) Introduction See also R.3.24n.B. Generally, see the Sarna and Roach books. There is much well-known law on when anappealwill not be heard because it is moot.There is also some on when that is or is not a bar to alawsuit, and when a declaration may be possible.

Where declaratory relief is sought, it cannot be said to be moot simply because no other form of relief is sought alongside it.Conversely, the possibility of granting a declaration is not determinative of whether a live controversy continues to exist. Declarations are mainly sought in commercial matters to help parties define their rights, or

assist in an amicable settlement.A declaration does not create rights;it clarifies existing rights. That may be done by interpreting an admitted contract or license. There is a vast body of case law about when the court will or will not give a declaratory judgment without any other relief, e.g., because it is too early for other relief. One can seek a

declaration by a statement of claim, and would have to do so if all the conditions of R.3.2(2) were not met.But such questions also arise very frequently under R.3.2(2). Declaratory relief is a matter of discretion for the court, and should not be granted lightly on appeal where the chambers judge declined to grant it.

(b) Tests The general power to give a declaration comes from theJudicature Act, not this Rule.There is no fixed list of when the court can or cannot give a declaration, but these criteria usually weigh heaviest: *whether the question is likely to arise and affect rights, so that a declaration would be of

Special constitutional and other considerations affect suits for declarations against the Crown, especially where normal suits against the Crown are not available, or where some of the bodies involved are not legal persons.One may sue the Attorney-General for a declaration when one wishes relief against the Crown, and the legislation on Crown claims does not apply.It is no bar that the declaration could also be useful in a later suit.

Even thoughthe court of one province may have no jurisdiction over the Crown in right of a different province, still a declaration may be appropriate, especially if there is no danger of contradictory findings by two courts in two provinces from the case pending in another province. Legislation on suits for declarations of constitutional validity (and no other relief), as in other provinces, allows suit only by the local or federal Attorney General, not by the Attorney General of another province.

*3.Miscellaneous Ordinarily a party cannot get relief or go into issues, which are not in his originating pleading, and so an Originating Application seeking only declaratory relief (and costs) will not permit them. It is doubtful that an attack on an arbitration award must be made under R.3.15 rather than

under R.3.2(2). A court which has only statutory appeal jurisdiction in respect of a certain type of administrative board's decision, cannot give a declaration. If it is desired to settle the law, a declaration may be more appropriate than a class action for large total damages.

The Attorney General sought a declaration which the other parties strongly contested.The court denied relief to the Attorney General, but gave a declaration to the opposite effect, apparently without any counterclaim, and with little discussion of this procedural point. Summary declarations of right are dangerous.

If an immediate declaration would likely cause serious disruption, or lead to bad decisions or relitigation, the court can postpone the effect of its declaration for a number of months (under R. 1.4, andJudicature Acts.8). On setting aside a decision instead of giving the declaration asked for, see R.3.24(1).

Pure declarations are not barred in Alberta by limitation periods, leading in belated situations to trying for mere declarations and debates over whether they are purely declaratory.

*D.Controverted Elections A provincial Legislature has exclusive and inherent jurisdiction to deal with election issues, and a court gets jurisdiction only to the extent that legislation gives it such powers.The court then is not using its ordinary civil or criminal jurisdiction.

Election appeals should be expedited. An order made in a contested election is not a final judgment, and may not even be a "judicial proceeding?" Time to challenge a municipal election running from "the election"does not start to run until

official declaration of the winner (unless it is by acclamation).If proceedings must be taken within x days after publication of election returns, they cannot be taken before publication. If the last day to issue a petition is a holiday, it can be done the next business day, because of theInterpretation Act. If a petition omits details such as residence, that is a curable objection to form. But absent

a special statutory provision, the court cannot waive statutory time limits for, or procedural preconditions to, an election challenge. Though a return to the Executive Council by a returning officer is final, it is wrong to send in such a return after service of a notice of appeal and recount, and doing that does not exclude the court's discretion?

The successful candidate in the election should be the respondent, even if the petition claims that he was not properly nominated and so not really a candidate. An election petition can claim both a different result (different M.P.) and a new election, and the judges trying the case can both recount the ballots and declare the election void for corruption or illegality.

*3.3(1)The appropriate judicial centre for the purpose of these rules is*(a)the closest judicial centre,by road,to the Alberta residences or*Alberta places of business of all the parties, or*(b)if a single judicial centre cannot be determined under clause (a),the*judicial centre that is,by road, the closest to the Alberta residence*or Alberta place of business of one of the parties, that party being
*selected by the party starting the action.*(2)If a party carries on business in more than one Alberta location, the place*of business in Alberta of that party for the purpose of this rule is the place of*business that is nearest to the location at which the matters in issue in the action*arose or were transacted.*(3)Despite subrules (1) and (2),the parties may agree on a judicial centre in
*which to start and carry on the action, in which case the judicial centre agreed*on by the parties is the appropriate judicial centre unless the Court otherwise*orders.*1 Defined Terms Court, judicial centre, party, rules

Related Provisions 3.2 (how to start an action); 3.4 (claimingland); 3.5 (moving action);3.6(2) (movement of motions and trials);6.11 (electronic hearings);8.18 (electronic trials);11.21 (electronic service); 13.41 (fax filing);14.8(5) (venue of appeals);14.84 (place of filing); 15.13 (transitional rule) Rule 3.3 sets out how to determine the appropriate judicial centre in which to commence and

carry on, or to transfer, an action.On when a suit once in the appropriate centre now should be moved to another, see R.3.5 and notes. The Rules were heavily amended in 1996, and pre-1996 cases giving the plaintiff's choice great weight are no longer law, but the new 2010 Rules make no real change to the 1996 Rules. No longer do the odd boundaries of judicialdistrictsgovern;now the test is distance to judicial

*centresby usual methods of travel. The Rules are designed to prevent a plaintiff's picking a venue for the suit which has no connection at all with the location of either party. If the relevant parties to the lawsuit clearly are in or close to one judicial centre, that is where the plaintiff must start the lawsuit.The defendants

have a right to have it moved there if it was not started there.That the non-resident plaintiff wants to run all its foreclosure actions in one judicial centre and use one counsel, is not enough to depart from the Rule. Calgary cannot be the proper judicial centre if both parties reside in Edmonton, and the court should transfer the suit under R.3.5, if R.3.3 was not complied with;the place in R. 3.3 is presumed correct.

*(a)the judicial centre that is closest,by road,to the land, or*(b)the judicial centre that is closest, by road,to the Alberta residence of*a defendant,*a defendant may, by making a request in Form 6,require the court clerk in*the judicial centre in which the action is located to transfer the action to the*judicial centre that is closest,by road,to the land or the Alberta residence of
*that defendant.

Information Note See also rule 3.10 [Application of Part 4 and Part 5].

Defined Terms court, rules Related Provisions 1.7(2) (Rules fill gaps by analogy);3.2(4) (wrong form of action) Legislation expressly letting a municipality have a summary hearing for an injunction does not mandate that procedure, and if the evidence conflicts and there are credibility issues, it is

appropriate to hold a hearing with evidence. There are many situations in which the court should direct trial of an issue. But at a late stage, absent any significant conflict of facts, and absent an order for other procedures, neither more pleadings, nor discovery, nor trial is proper.A special chambers

application should be held on the merits. The mere fact that the claim is for a declaration of constitutional rights does not give the respondent the right to have the Originating Application converted into an orthodox suit by statement of claim.

The procedure to follow to try an issue is not well known.The court should either itself define the precise questions to be tried, or should direct the exchange of pleadings.And it should direct who has the onus of proof. If the court directs pleadings, then the party who has the onus of proof should file a document called"Statement of the Applicant's Claim".This isnota statement of claim, does not start a new proceeding, uses the action number of the existing suit (Originating

Application), and there is no fee for filing it. It has no notice to defendant at the back, but otherwise alleges facts much the way that a statement of claim would.Its prayer for relief may be somewhat different.And at the end it does not say that it was issued, and the Clerk does not sign or seal it. By analogy, pleadings in an Originating Application should be deemed closed on the date of

the original scheduled hearing, and so if the material raised an extra issue, it also was properly before the court. *Questioning on Affidavit and QuestioningWitnesses*3.13(1)The following persons may be questioned by a party adverse in interest:*(a)a person who makes an affidavit in support of an originating

Court, enactment, expert, party, record Related Provisions 3.13 (questioning on an affidavit) A respondent objecting to the short time for an (originating) application has a duty to tell the judge of its objection, and that it wants an adjournment to cross-examine on an affidavit.It is too late to wait until the judge has decided the motion.

After a report to the court by a court-appointed Inspector of a company, the court should not order his disclosure of back-up documents used to compile his report.He should not become an information-gathering tool for any party, especially if non-parties could suffer from disclosure. 1 Onhabeas corpus, see R.3.16. 2 Onhabeas corpus, see also R.3.16.Paterson v.Skate Can.2004 ABQB 969, 373 AR 81;but cf.Rankin *v. Alta.Curling Fedn.Appeals Ctee.2005 ABQB 938, 390 AR 246. 3A superior court (despite absence of Rules on prerogative writs in criminal cases) can issuemandamusto

The topic here is civil judicial review of the actions of governments and administrative tribunals. At common law, that was called the prerogative writs ofhabeas corpus, certiorari, prohibition, subject *mandamus, andquo warranto. In Alberta for many years those remedies continued, only to a largely cosmetic change:splitting each writ into a notice of motion and an order of the

same name.But since 1987, they have all been combined into this single simple procedure, judicial review, with injunctions and declarations added. In principle, such procedural legislation did not change the old substantive grounds for the writs ofcertiorarietc.But coincidentally, around the same time, the Supreme Court of Canada modified those substantive grounds.

There are general textbooks on judicial review. *B.Discretion Unlike actions for debt, negligence, etc., most forms of prerogative relief or judicial review do not ordinarily lie as of right.They are said to be"discretionary", and an applicant with clearly proven cause of action may be refused relief for good reason. Presumably the grounds for such

refusal are broad. Judicial review of interlocutory decisions of tribunals is discouraged.Judicial review should occur after exhaustion of internal appeals, particularly where the final appeal is to the Court of Appeal.One litigant cannot litigate by instalments. *3.Technical Flaws
6Whose authorities are reviewed as well:McIver v.Ethics Comm'r.2018 ABQB 240, JCC 1701 02666 (Mar

Another ground for exercising the discretion not to grant relief is that the defect complained of is only technical, and has not created any substantial wrong or miscarriage of justice.That is now enacted by R.3.24(3), which is analogous to R.14.75, and codifies the discretion to withhold relief where giving relief would be unjust, e.g., for a trivial flaw. *C.Miscellaneous
Judicial review is a public law remedy only available against public bodies exercising public functions, and not against private organizations. One King's Bench judge cannot judicially review the decision of another, even if the decision was given as part of a statutory function.The doctrine ofpersona designatais gone in Alberta, and the remedy is an appeal.

A person or group given statutory powers can be a party to judicial review, even if that person or group has no legal personality. The courts cannot conduct judicial review of decisions by the Legislature, its Speaker, or its Ethics Commissioner, because of parliamentary immunity.

An issue of private law, like contract, does not become a matter for judicial review just because a public body is involved. The doctrine of collateral attack does not bar a suit against thefederal government (especially in a provincial superior court) for damages, even if whatever act was complained of by the plaintiff had not been quashed on judicial review by the Federal Court.The superior court has full power

to remove its power or jurisdiction.An act by a Minister can be wrong and breach of contract or an abuse, without being a nullity. The rules aboutforum non conveniensrelate to private law litigation, not to professional disciplinary proceedings or to appeals from or judicial review of that. *D.Tests and Standards*1.Certiorari
The general tests forcertiorari(an order quashing a decision) are discussed in the Civil Procedure Encyclopedia. It or similar judicial review does not lie against a private contractual officer. A classic use ofcertiorari(judicial review) is to quash a decision flowing from breach of the

rules of natural justice, such as the adjudicator looking at electronic maps not in evidence, and (without notice or a chance to reply) using them to reach a decision.Such decisions have no standard of review.The fairness requirement was breached and that settles the matter. *2.Mandamus Some general principles ofmandamus(ordering a body to carry out its duties) are reviewed.

*Mandamusagainst a public officer is not equitable relief. After an appeal, which the statute says is final, one cannot move formandamusto force the official appealed from to (re)hear the matter. *Mandamusdoes not lie against the Crown or the Queen's agents, and only lies against
someone with a legal duty.

6-month time limit. *5.Standard of Review TheVavilovtest of reasonableness on judicial review itself means a decision based on an internally coherent and rational chain of analysis and that is justified in relation to the facts and law that constrain the decision maker.It is not enough for the outcome of the decision to be justifiable;

it must also be justified by way of the reasons given by the decision maker.The court reviewing must develop an understanding of the decisions maker's reasoning process to see if the decision as a whole is reasonable.The hallmarks of reasonableness are justification, transparency, and intelligibility.And the decision must be justified in relation to the relevant factual and legal constraints bearing on the decision.

*4.Prayer *5.Stay andTransfer of File See also R.3.23n. Stay of the order impugned pending judicial review follows the usual 3-part test for interlocutory injunctions.In the second two parts, if a government authority is involved, public interest is a special

factor.The 3 parts of the test are not air-tight compartments, and to a degree strength in one can compensate for weakness in another, especially as the second and third parts are inexorably linked and should be considered together.At the end, a fundamental question is whether a stay would be just and equitable in all the circumstances. There are examples of what is and is not irreparable harm, and the public interest in balancing (in)convenience.

The Originating Application for judicial review does more than most documents which start a lawsuit.It also suspends jurisdiction of the inferior body if the Application seeks the equivalent of prohibition, i.e.an order stopping the inferior body from proceeding. And where the Originating Application seeks to quash a decision, it bears a special notice to the inferior body under R.3.18,

and compels that body to send in a"return"attaching all the documents which it holds in the Understandable delays of a few months in judicial review or administrative appeal were looked at using the usual 3-step approach (derived from interlocutory injunction procedure) and found not bad enough to end the stay of the order below.

If the time expires before service on a necessary party vitally affected, the problem is incurable and judicial review is impossible. Therefore filing and service within six months is vital.That takes more time than does drafting a statement of claim, so filing shortly before the six months expires sometimes is hard to avoid.But filing live at the Clerk's counter is often not allowed now, and certain types of electronic filing may not occur until sometime after the document is electronically

submitted to the Clerk.This can be a grave danger.See details in R.13.14n., and in long notes in R.3.2n.B.4.But serving a copy without a filing endorsement or stamp is not a nullity, especially if the Clerk will not give one within the time limit for service, so then one can serve in time an unendorsed copy and later serve an endorsed copy. But can an originating document received by the Clerk in time but only processed later outside

the time limit, be ordered by the court to be viewed as"filed"in time? Rule 3.15(2) cannot be waived. The six-month time limit is no bar to amending an Originating Notice for judicial review to add a constitutional argument not in it before? But the time limit does bar adding a new party later.

Ordinarily the tribunal whose decision is being reviewed cannot be heard on the merits, only on the scope of its jurisdiction. Concerns about participation by a tribunal in judicial review or an appeal from itself are substantial, but the ban on tribunal participation is not absolute.It may be allowed to intervene if otherwise the proceeding would be unopposed;but if there are competent

parties able to do so, then its participation is less likely.Concerns about the impartiality or partiality of the tribunal taking part depend in part whether it is adjudicating conflicts between individuals, or whether it is making policy, or regulating or investigating.It is bad if a tribunal not only intervenes but tries to supplement and shore up its earlier decision by giving more arguments on appeal, particularly if the parties were not warned of that possible argument during the hearing before the tribunal. In judicial review, the decision-maker cannot be examined on the decision itself (as

distinguished from other matters outside the decision-making process) without a better foundation to suggest infringement of a rule. On costs against the tribunal, see R.2.10n.E. A public official sued for judicial review (here a Cabinet Minister) may not technically be a tribunal, and in any event the particular statute and particular lack of opposing parties, may put

that officer at the ministerial end of the scale, owing no dutyto be neutral So he may be entitled to claim costs, if persons with no real standing sued for judicial review and then appealed when they fail. A public body with special insight and an interest in process, was allowed to intervene.

A statutory body exercising a statutory power has enough legal status to be a respondent and subject to judicial review.

*Habeas corpusis rightly regarded as almost sacred, and a cornerstone of the subject's defence against the monarch (government).Such applications are presumed to be urgent. The historic remedy ofhabeas corpusis very important, and exceptions to the right to get it are restrictively construed (such as a statutory remedy said to be as good).

*B.Federal Court Generally, the Federal Court of Canada has exclusive jurisdiction over judicial review of federal government bodies. which provincial superior courts can But that is not so ofhabeas corpus, hear and grant, if the prisoner is held within that province.

It would take very clear language to take away from the provincial superior courts the power to granthabeas corpus, and theFederal Court Actdoes not do so, so the Federal Court of Canada and the provincial superior courts have overlapping jurisdiction. Where an inmate in a federal penitentiary alleges that his liberty is improperly interfered with, e.g.by improper administrative segregation, he can challenge that either by judicial review in the

Federal Court, orhabeas corpusin a provincial superior court.The alternative remedy in the Federal Court is not enough reason for the superior court to decline to hearhabeas corpuson the merits. The superior court can so decline if there is a statutory right of appeal, or if there is *corpusin the provincial superior courts, and it has some advantages.

*Notice to Obtain Record of Proceedings*3.18(1) An originating applicant for judicial review who seeks an order to set*aside a decision or act must include with the originating application a notice in*Form 8,addressed to the person or body who made or possesses the record*of proceedings on which the decision or act sought to be set aside is based, to*send the record of proceedings to the court clerk named in the notice.
*(2)The notice must require the following to be sent or an explanation of why an*item cannot be sent:*(a)the written record, if any,of the decision or act that is the subject of*the originating application for judicial review,*(b)the reasons given for the decision or act,if any,*(c)the document which started the proceeding,
*(d)the evidence and exhibits filed with the person or body,if any, and*(e)anything else relevant to the decision or act in the possession of the*person or body.*(3)The Court may add to, dispense with or vary anything required to be sent to*the court clerk under this rule.*3
Defined Terms Court, court clerk At one time, the writ ofcertiorarihad two effects.First, it immediately moved the record (both physically, and notionally the whole proceeding) from the inferior body to the superior court.And second, it led to a hearing at which the superior court would examine the record and ultimately decide whether or not to quash the decision below.The first of those two effects is now covered

by Rules 3.18 ff. Where the Originating Application seeks to set aside a decision or act, the applicant has the right and duty to have the inferior body physically send up its record to King's Bench.That is demanded by a special endorsement on the Originating Application, and its service on the inferior body.Where the Originating Application does not seek to set aside a decision, but some other relief (e.g.prohibition), R.3.20 allows King's Bench to order the record below sent up.

Generally speaking, the scope of relevance for the return is broad, and is not confined to matters listed in R.3.18(2).

*1 Defined Terms Court, court clerk, prescribed form This is about how to comply with R.3.18. The tribunal has to send up the original of its complete record.The tribunal must send all papers in its return under R.3.19, and destroy none.

There is no requirement to put the certified copy ("return") in sworn form, but it should be certified.How the contents are organized is a housekeeping matter for the King's Bench judge in charge, and a Court of Appeal should not interfere with that question. create a verbatim transcript from a statutory tribunal, and affidavits can be used to show whether there was evidence of something. The return need not contain papers subject to solicitor-client privilege, even if they were

A tribunal has"deliberative"secrecy for the content of and substance of its decision making and deliberations, but not for the formal process which it established to ensure consistency. Where a tribunal learns that judicial review of its decision is pending, it should not of its own

motion give a reconsideration as a way of producing additional reasons to shore up its original reasons.

*B.Declarations On the purpose and limits on granting declarations, see R.3.2 n.C. The grounds for a pure declaration by the court are narrow (and there are 4 or 5 tests for it). It is not normally given if the dispute is over and academic, or has not yet arisen and may not arise.

Where declaratory relief is sought, it cannot be said to be moot simply because no other form of relief is sought alongside it. The person seeking the declaration needs a legal interest or right in jeopardy or grave else's, is not enough.

An estate beneficiary may not have the right to seek advice and directions from the court, but the Surrogate Rules allow those interested to seek directions.It may be treated as a motion for a declaration. Many Canadian courts have power to consider the constitutionality of legislation, and

provincial superior courts have inherent jurisdiction over both federal and provincial matters, including constitutionality.If extra-territorial effects are alleged, those challenging constitutionality of a statute need not go to the province which enacted it, to question its validity, so long as their local courts have a legitimate ground to consider its effects.A declaration is possible. *C.Reconsideration If the court finds a legal flaw, but cannot tell whether it had or should have any effect, the court has to send the matter back to the tribunal to reconsider. Rule 3.24(3) can be used to overlook an error by the tribunal which did not affect the outcome. But if there is only one rational

solution to the question before the tribunal, sending the matter back to reconsider is pointless and dangerous, and the court should make the decision. There is a discussion of what is fairness or unfairness and its link to natural justice.The more the tribunal's process, its nature, and the things it must decide, resemble judicial decision making, the more likely it is that procedural protections closer to the trial model will be required by the duty

so is whether to permit cross-examination.Usually cross-examination on affidavits for a pending hearing is allowed, and that is even more so when some of the issues are complex or difficult. Another aspect of fairness is whether the party has enough information and time to defend himself. A matter was sent back to a labor arbitration board to reconsider.

Where a matter is sent back to the tribunal to reconsider, there is no fixed rule about whether a new panel or the original one should decide it.Fairness and its appearance may have to be balanced against practicality and efficiency. *D. Costs
A number of criteria for the scale of costs on judicial review are given (with a review of some case law):length, importance to parties and public, whether winner had any personal interest, and novelty of issues. *Subdivision 2 Time Limit for Service of Statement of Claim *Time for Service of Statement of Claim*3.26(1) A statement of claim must be served on the defendant within one year*after the date that the statement of claim is filed unless the Court,on application*filed before the one year time limit expires,grants an extension of time for

Beware of this Rule! Delay in serving a statement of claim can make a plaintiff's claim disappear.If a statement of claim is not served, and if the time is not extended within one year of the anniversary of its issue, and if the limitation period of the claim has also expired, usually the action and cause of action in effect are dead, and the plaintiff can take no steps in the suit. See R.3.28.This Rule exists to help both parties.

the original year only in very narrow circumstances.These changes attest to the purpose of this Rule:to prevent delay and encourage expedient litigation.So decisions on the Rules before 1991 Because of the post-1991 restrictions on when a renewal application can be brought, lengthy delays in service are no longer possible.On the tests for renewal, see R.3.27. The consequences of R.3.26 are neither optional nor discretionary:they are mandatory.

One cannot extend time to serve a statement of claim except by a valid timely order to renew the statement of claim. Apart from specific exceptions in Rule 3.27, once a statement of claim expires, it cannot be renewed or validly served, despite the plaintiff's hardship, lack of blame, or other fairness considerations. The test for renewal within the year is easier, but after the year

expires, renewal (under R.3.27) faces far stiffer tests. The test under R.3.26 is reviewed:the big requirement is that it cannot be for the purpose of delay;and there cannot be serious prejudice to the defendant.

a previous order for substitutional service, dispensing with service, or validating service, is set aside. *B.Renewal The criteria to extend time are reviewed. The test for renewing a statement of claim outside

the first year (R.3.27) is much tougher than for renewal within that year (R.3.26). Within one year of issue, renewal of the statement of claim is fairly easy to get, if proper evidence is given? Prejudice to the defendant is unlikely because even if a renewal is granted, the only effect of R.3.26(2) is to permit service within 15 months of a statement of claim being

issued, rather than 12 months.The expiry of a limitation period before the renewal being sought cannot in itself be relied upon to establish prejudice.Sworn evidence of attempts to serve, and why there would be service lacking within 12 months, are needed for an extension.Some previous attempt to serve is needed.The evidence should let the court infer that the renewal request is not simply a tactic for delay.There were 45 defendants, time to serve was during the Covid pandemic, and some service had been made.Evidence let one conclude that this was not a stalling tactic,

even if no specific evidence about the appellant defendants. Under R.3.27(1)(c), a late extension of time to serve a statement of claim requires special or extraordinary circumstances, and some causal connection between the acts of persons not connected to the plaintiff, and the failure to serve on time. Rule 1.4 creates no exception to that

precondition. Inadvertence by the plaintiff's lawyer is not a ground. But it may be the Clerk's neglect in processing a filed application for a time extension. At this stage, mere lack of prejudice is not enough.

Any pleading must be filed and served within the deadlines set by statutes and Rules.In Alberta it is customary to file first any pleading after a statement of claim, then serve it.Statutory limitation of actions periods set the deadlines to issue a statement of claim.The time to serve a statement of claim is set by R.3.26, and the time to serve a third party notice is set by R.3.45. Usually the time to defend against a statement of claim or third party notice served within

Alberta is 20 days, but one month to defend against a claim served outside Alberta, and 2 months if served outside Canada.Under Rr.13.3 and 13.4, one excludes the partial day at the beginning (i.e., the day of service) in computing these times.The times can be varied before or after they expire, under R.13.5.It is not hard to get most of these times extended, unless default judgment or the like has occurred in themeantime (for then evidence of merit is needed). But times set by

statutes (and by a few express Rules) cannot be extended. It is very common for defence lawyers to get from the plaintiff's lawyers an agreed extension of time to file and serve a statement of defence.Indeed, lawyers for plaintiffs should not refuse such requests without good reason.The mere impatience of the plaintiff is not enough reason to refuse an extension. On the other hand, the defence must realize that it cannot take advantage of any times which

expire during such an agreed extension. Since statements of claim are often served just before the limitation period expires, the defence solicitor should immediately carefully determine whether the right parties have been named, and whether the obvious causes of action have been pleaded. If they have not, a defence should be promptly drafted and served, without even seeking any extension of time to defend.That way the plaintiff cannot argue that any limitations defence have

been waived. Oddly, the lawyers for both sides frequently do a very poor job of wording or recording agreed time extensions.Sometimes they do it by telephone with no written confirmation.Often letter writers become coy, and use very indirect language.Instead, one should write plainly:"I ask that

12.11 (defence and demand for notice in family law);12.12 (time for service in family law litigation);13.3, 13.4 (calculating time) England and some provinces (not Alberta) start a suit with a writ of summons requiring the defendant to file only a simple appearance document to prevent default judgment at that stage. Alberta's partial equivalent to the appearance is the demand for notice (formerly "demand of notice").It is used in Alberta only by someone who has no defence to liability, but wants notice of

later proceedings, such as an application for judgment.It prevents default judgment by the Clerk. A demand for notice does not give the right to contest liability (unless the court later allows the defendant to withdraw the demand and substitute a statement of defence ).A defendant who does not file and serve a statement of defence admits liability.The demand for notice is common where the only dispute will be about the timing and terms of the relief, especially in foreclosures.

It could be used in a damage action where the only issue is assessing the amount of damages, but defendants even in such actions often file a statement of defence. A demand for notice is a virtual invitation to the plaintiff to move for summary judgment (which is what a foreclosure order nisi/sale is). *Judgment or Order by Agreement*3.35(1) If a lawyer files a statement of defence or demand for notice on behalf of

*A.Cross-References On structured settlements and periodical payments, see R.9.1n.On amending offers to settle, see theNewellcase. *B.Proving Consent Rule 3.35(1) is designed to prevent an opponent from going behind the back of a lawyer and

making a deal directly with the lawyer's client.The Law Society's Code of Conduct also prevents a lawyer from communicating directly with another lawyer's client. A consent order or judgment needs the consent of the opposing lawyer unambiguously stated; it is often endorsed on the order.A settlement of pending litigation, made through the two lawyers, is not invalid just because it lacks the formalities in ss.37, 38 of theMatrimonial Property Act.

To get a proper consent order or judgment, every party to the suit should consent (by its solicitor of record).And if some non-party may be adversely affected, its consent should be obtained, or the justice or applications judge should be clearly informed of the situation, in writing. If a non-consenting party has been noted in default, the situation is complicated, but at least the proposed order or judgment should be consistent with the statement of claim which was served.

Failure to do any of those things leaves the lawyer getting the order open to later having the order set aside, and being accused of sharp practice or worse. The Administrator of theMotor Vehicle

Related Provisions 3.68(1)(a) (striking out defence);9.15(3) (setting aside default) This default judgment Rule was amended in 2020 to make it subject to the new Rules on service under the Hague Convention on Service Abroad. *A.Introduction On this general topic, see theTweedie textbook.

In order to prevent being noted in default or suffering default judgment, a defendant (or defendant by counterclaim or third party) should both file and serve, a statement of defence (or a demand for notice) within the time limited. There is some discussion of what is an appearance or a defence, in theLindblomcase.

*B.Computing Time Under R.13.3, one computes time to defend by excluding the partial day of service.The next day is day number one of the time to defend.The defendant has all of the 20th day to defend. Once the time to defend has expired without service (or without filing) of a defence, a race is on.The plaintiff can note default (or sign default judgment) and the defendant can file and serve

a defence.The one who does it first wins. But plaintiff's counsel has a duty to grant reasonable time requests to avoid a foot race to the courthouse. If both lawyers come to the clerk's counter at the same time, and the plaintiff's lawyer insists upon noting default, the court may well set it aside with costs, and without proof of a meritorious defence.

Defined Terms claim, costs award, court clerk, defendant, judgment, order, party, plaintiff Related Provisions

3.68 (striking deficient claims) How does a plaintiff get to judgment if a defendant does not file or serve a statement of defence and part or all of the claim against him is unliquidated, e.g., damages? Of course the matter can just be sent to"assessment" in the usual way:a trial with live evidence by a judge on issues of quantum (not liability).That is R.3.37(3)(c), and is really just inherent in the court's usual procedure.But R.3.37 gives alternative simpler procedures which can be used if the amounts

are smaller, or if the matter is not very contentious.For example, sometimes a claim technically is unliquidated, but there is not much doubt how to compute it. If pleadings of the defendant are struck out, the plaintiff cannot enter a money judgment in an unliquidated case without proof of amount, and instead should go to assessment. Very often the loss sustained can be assessed from affidavits, without live evidence.

For constitutional and statutory reasons, whether an Applications Judge can assess damages is now unsettled. However, in some cases of disputed facts, it might be more efficient for a justice to order a reference to an Applications Judge to take evidence and report back on damages, to be confirmed by a judge.

If part of the suit is for a liquidated claim and part is for an unliquidated claim, the plaintiff should be able to sign judgment for the liquidated part and have the rest reserved and put down for assessment. Where a statement of claim claims two alternative remedies, though the defendant is noted in default, he should be given notice of a motion to pick one remedy.Default admits liability, but not

which remedy, especially if legal issues are involved. A judgment by one judge under R.3.37, after noting all the defendants in default, without live evidence, and no evidence at all as regards one defendant, and affidavit evidence re the other defendants, may be a judgment by default.If so, the Rules let another judge set it aside.

A defendant noted in default cannot raise a limitations defence, because it has to be pled, despite R.3.37.

*A.Default Judgment Default judgment can be signed only for a debtor or"liquidated demand".Rule 3.39(2) defines that, and the test is whether an exact or easily calculable amount is sued for.If a contract calls for payment of a fixed amount, such as a lump sum, that is liquidated.Similarly, if it calls for a price per barrel or per ton, and the plaintiff sues for a precise number of barrels or tons delivered;that is a liquidated claim.A liquidated debt is one whose amount can be found by calculation, or fixed

by any scale or charges or other positive data, not by opinion, assessment, what is reasonable, or depends on the circumstances of the case. Damages are not liquidated. With wording similar to R.3.39(2)(a), "debt"or specific sum of money fixed by the terms of a contract did not cover a reimbursement claim under travel medical insurance, because the insurance was expressly conditional on the government having enough funds left over from funds

granted by the Legislative Assembly. There can be default judgment for solicitor-client (full indemnity) costs, if that is contracted for and sued for. O.27 r.2.This Rule was similar to 1914 R.152 (first part), except for the reference to a demand of notice, and

(c)Facts and Evidence Prejudgment interest should be based on evidence. So should any denial of interest because of the plaintiff's delay. The Court can award prejudgment interest higher than is named in the Judgment Interest Act, but that would need proof of loss of investment revenue, an agreement

for compound interest, or impropriety or bad faith. If a contract or bill of exchange gives a rate of interest, that usually applies, not the statutory prejudgment interest rate. Prejudgment interest applies to special damages also. If a contract is silent as to interest, but the court finds the

circumstances imply a term as to interest, in a commercial transaction it may be at a commercial rate, which was taken to be that in s.4(2) of theJudgment Interest Act. (d)When Interest Starts and Ends Prejudgment interest is to be calculated from the date that the cause of action arose.

Prejudgment interest for negligent misrepresentation or advice, only runs from when the damage accrues. If the suit is On when to begin calculating interest on a loan, see theMatthisoncase. for breach of contract, it runs from the date of breach. Where damages are assessed as of date of judgment, there is no reason to award pre-

judgment interest. Prejudgment interest is usually awarded only on the part of the damages which accrued up to the date of judgment.There cannot be prejudgment interest on pecuniary loss identified as arising after judgment, such as loss of capacity to earn in future. And if post- judgment interest is at a different rate, the court must calculate it on each part of the damages,

even before any overlap between the different heads of damage is deducted. Past lost earning capacity may be treated as a lost even stream of income, with interest calculated at 6-month intervals. If the breach continued or grew for some time, the amount owing may be calculated as of each January 1.

(g)Compound Interest Compound prejudgment interest requires special grounds, but may be and must be pleaded, proper in many circumstances, such as the defendant earning compound interest or the plaintiff deprived of such likely earnings.Where a contract says nothing about interest, one cannot say

that the parties agreed on compound interest or knew that it was necessary.Royalty payments may typically be made over a long time and people may gain such rights as an investment, but it would take evidence of that, and a judge is not bound to take judicial notice of that and therefore

award compound interest. (h) Miscellaneous

A renewed judgment cannot capitalize interest in the old judgment, as that would lead to compound interest? Though statute allows prejudgment interest, the plaintiff normally should include a request for that in its pleadings, though a late amendment to do that may be possible.A request for such an

amendment is not hopeless if the evidence already before the court supports such an interest award. Where local legislation gives a discount rate to calculate damages as a lump sum, it applies in all cases, and cannot be adjusted for investment expenses, nor for the trial judge's idea of what the actual rate of return on investments would be.A damages award is to be final, and the trial

judge should not later adjust it to reflect what the plaintiff has spent a portion of the damages upon. A creditor should not sue for a declaration of money owing, and so keep the higher contractual rate of interest running even after judgment.An order declaring validity of, and to enforce, liens is a"judgment" under theJudgment Interest Actwhich changes (drops) interest from the contractual

rate to the statutory rate on judgments. An express interest award in a foreign judgment cannot be ignored or altered in a suit here on that foreign judgment. 1It is not enough that there used to be monthly payments:James H. MeekTr.v. San Juan Res.2005 ABCA

defendant, judgment, plaintiff What if some defendants defend and others do not? Rules 3.37, 3.38 and 3.39 expressly allow the plaintiff to enter default judgment against those who did not defend, and then go on with the lawsuit against those who did.That may not have been possible at common law, but this subrule presumably reverses that.Rule 15.2(1) means that the 2010 Rules apply if after default judgment

against one defendant the suit continued against other defendants. However, there is a large exception to R.3.40.New Rr.3.39 and 3.40 make doubtful the old rule that one cannot take judgment against the principal and his agent, and that taking judgment against one bars judgment against the other.But that rule of election still holds if the two are only alternatively liable. One can use R.3.40 only if the defendants are concurrently liable, e.g., if

they have signed a joint and several note, or one is a debtor and the other the guarantor (or they are sued in one statement of claim for different debts).If two defendants arealternativelyliable, a plaintiff who signs judgment against one elects to abandon the claim against the other.One example of such alternative liability is (or was) when principal and agent are the two defendants. Another common (overlapping) example is a case where it is not clear whether the goods were

bought by the business corporation or by the individual who seemed to be the proprietor.Where two people could only bealternativelyliable to pay the money, taking judgment against one bars a claim against the other.The court should itself refuse judgment against both.Taking judgment against the mortgagor necessarily asserted that the monies which he had paid to a third person were not paid on account of the mortgage, and so barred a claim against the payee to account for them;merger is not the reason, the causes of action being different.

If default judgment is given against two defendants who are not alternately liable, and one gets it opened up, the plaintiff has not elected to take judgment against one, and the one who opened up is not thereby freed of liability.Especially if the contract says that liability is joint and several.

claim, defendant, file, party, plaintiff *A.Alternatives A defendant need not use this Subdivision.The defendant can wait and see if it is held liable at trial. If it is, then the defendant can very often sue other people (whether or not they were parties to the lawsuit), claiming against them part or all of the original defendant's liability in the

first suit.For instance, a retailer sued by a customer for defective goods could sue the wholesaler who supplied them. But that defendant retailer would have to pay the plaintiff long before it could get indemnity from the new suit, and the retailer would have to prove the defect all over again against the wholesaler.Indeed, the two trials might produce opposite results on that topic, especially if some evidence was unavailable for one trial.Rule 3.44 is designated to avoid those problems:it allows

both lawsuits to be run together with a single trial. The mechanism is a third party notice, making the defendant a new plaintiff against the third party. *B.New Wide Test The 2010 Alberta Rules are now much wider than before.Under the 1968 Rule, the claim had

to be the same in the statement of claim and in the third party notice;connected claims were not enough. Defined Terms

claim, Court, defendant, party, plaintiff, third party defendant, third party plaintiff Related Provisions 3.56(2) (counterclaim by third party defendant);3.74 (adding parties) Rule 3.46 gives a third party full status as a party to the main suit. On the implications of that for costs, see theNicklescase. Counsel for third parties do not always take full advantage of that

status;sometimes they merely defend the third party proceeding and do not try to defend the main suit as allowed by R.3.49(1)(c). Usually a plaintiff who fails does not pay costs of third party proceedings, unless joinder of the third party was required and inevitable. The right level of Schedule C (i.e.which column) is set by

the amount which the statement of claim claims, and that may apply also to costs payable to a third party.A third party notice exposes the third parties to liability for the sum claimed, and the test for their costs is whether the third-party notice followed naturally and inevitably from the main action, i.e.whether the defendant had no real alternative but to join the third parties.The plaintiff sued the developer, who had all work done by contractors so it was inevitable they would be third-partied.

A successful plaintiff may get costs from the defendant occasioned by the defendant's third party notice. If the third party only comes into the suit because of the defendant's counterclaim, which fails, the defendant may have to pay any costs which the plaintiff incurs in relation to the third party.

Third party proceedings can become complex, especially if there are fourth and fifth parties. Who goes first at trial, and how to avoid duplication, become contentious.The court's directions are useful, even necessary, especially if the third and fourth party proceedings are slowing a simple main action, or if there is some reason that the entire thing should not be dealt with in a single trial.

Defined Terms

defendant, plaintiff, pleading, rules, third party defendant, third party plaintiff

A third party notice is a pleading, and must recite facts giving a cause of action, just as a statement of claim must. Part of a third party notice raising a claim outside the limitation period shouldbe "struck." Another part shown by affidavit to be hopeless should be struck too. Third party notices can be summarily dismissed. But it takes a clear case to strike out a third party

notice. A third party notice can be struck out if it does not disclose a cause of action by the defendant against the third party. The tests would be those under R.3.68.Presumably the court would also

strike it out if it violated R.3.45 and no time extension were given. A grant of summary judgment, even on a third party notice, is entitled to considerable deference on appeal. The Rules on formal offers and double costs to successful defendants who beat their own offers, apply to third party notices and offers by third parties.

Case law on whether a third party can counterclaim is unclear, but if he can, he has to name the person issuing the third party notice as a defendant by counterclaim.A counterclaim was struck for want of that. This Rule still appears not to allow an amendment to a counterclaimto add an extra plaintiff

The court can allow a late counterclaim. Relevant to such a request for a late counterclaim are whether it would greatly increase the time and cost of the main suit, and whether the defendant delayed after getting the relevant information which he could use to sue on. So the mere fact that part or all of the new counterclaim's subject is not related to the existing claim is not necessarily

Though the test for a late counterclaim is fairly low, it is tougher if the counterclaim will a bar. allege fraud or malicious conduct:the case law on amending pleadings gives guidance. And of course the court will not allow a late counterclaim to the extent that the limitation period forsuing had expired.

Limitations periods do not bar a counterclaim related to the main claim, but it may not be possible to amend a statement of defence to raise that mere argument. Adding a new counterclaim where there was no counterclaim before is an amendment of the original lawsuit or pleading, and subject to the usual rules for amending pleadings, and if there is a limitations issue, subject to the

usual principles there including how to apply and interpret s.6 of theLimitations Act.

*Status of Counterclaim*3.58 A counterclaim is an independent action. Defined Terms 3.60 ( application of Rules);9.10 (judgment on counterclaim) Though a counterclaim may to some extent adopt the facts in the statement of defence, still it is a distinct pleading and must comply with the Rules requiring that a pleading set out the fundamental

allegations. Some counterclaims will expressly incorporate the words in the statement of defence, and if so, both those pleadings must be considered for jurisdiction purposes. The previous Rules contemplated that the counterclaim would be tried with the claim, unless they were severed.The criteria for doing that were not technical, but practical and somewhat

flexible. If the two claims are linked, as where the defendant claims set-off, then the counterclaim should not be tried separately. The position is less clear now that a counterclaim isan "independent action".If a set-off is claimed under R.3.59, it will still often be convenient to try them together if they are related.Though the Rules say a counterclaim is a separate action, it and the main

claim are not treated as unrelated for all purposes.The grounds for security in the Rules are not exhaustive. A plaintiff who sues in Alberta attorns to the jurisdiction and becomes subject to a counterclaim. The court refused to sever or stay the counterclaim on grounds of inappropriate forum. If a foreign

company sues here, our courts have jurisdiction to hear a counterclaim against it, and service on the solicitors for the plaintiff (defendant by counterclaim) is fit service within the jurisdiction. Where one side gets partial summary judgment but there is a pending counterclaim from the other side, the test for whether to stay execution on that one judgment until the counterclaim is decided, is like the test for a stay pending trial or pending appeal.

*Claiming Set-off*3.59 A matter that might be claimed by set-off may be claimed by counterclaim*or by pleading set-off as a defence.*6 Defined Terms pleading

Related Provisions 3.31 (form of defence) *A.General Any kind of set-off (if valid) is a defence. A set-off may now be pleaded either as a defence or as a counterclaim.The big difference is

that if set-off is pleaded only as a defence, then no judgment can be entered for the defendant if it turns out that the set-off exceeds the original claim.There is no requirement to plead a defence of set-off in order to establish equitable set-off, because the language of Rule 3.59 is permissive, and Rule 13.6(3) (which sets out the matters which must be specifically pleaded) does not list set-off.

*B.Legal Set-off
A party can contract out of the right to equitable set-off. Equitable set-off is subject to equitable defences, e.g.inequitable result where there is a bankruptcy with guarantees. All the usual equitable defences and restrictions apply to restrict equitable set-off. Equitable set-off does not require continuing mutuality, but it must have existed at some stage.

Stay because of a pending suit is not equitable set-off, and the criteria for that, such as mutuality, need not be met for a stay (even though sometimes they have much the same results).

2000 ABQB 529, 269 AR 79,revd[2001] 6WWR 265, 283 AR 4;Re SemCans.Crude Co. (#1)2009 ABCA

At common law there was no set-off, but several old English statutes in force in Alberta direct that cross-debts between the same parties are to be netted out.Therefore, each such debt is a defence (to the extent of its size) against the other.That is what is called legal set-off. Of course if the owner of the bigger debt is the defendant, it needs a counterclaim or suit, to recover the excess.Set-off under those statutes only applies to debts, i.e., liquidated claims. PLEADINGS AND CLOSE OF PLEADINGS *Request for Particulars*3.61(1) A party on whom a pleading is served may serve on the party who served*the pleading a request for particulars about anything in the pleading.*(2)If the requesting party does not receive a sufficient response within 10 days*after the date on which the request is served,the requesting party may apply to
*the Court for an order requiring the party who served the pleading to provide the*particulars.*(3)If the Court orders particulars to be provided,it must specify a time within*which the order is to be complied with.*(4)Subject to any order,despite a request for particulars,the obligation under*these rules to file and serve pleadings continues even though a request for
*particulars has been made and whether or not it has been complied with. Defined Terms file, order, party, pleading, rules Related Provisions 3.68 (deficient claims);13.6 (contents of pleadings);13.7 (required particulars) *A.General
There is a review of the various purposes for particulars.Giving or demanding or ordering particulars is a pragmatic and discretionary process. Where a pleading does not give enough details, the opposing party may apply to have the court compel the offending party to give further and better particulars. A party may demand and get further and better particulars in two cases.One is where R.13.7

or another analogous Rule has been violated.The other is where further pleading or discovery or trial will be impossible, unduly difficult, unfair, or expensive, without such particulars. It is customary to give particulars of negligence, and the court may order particulars if they are not yet pleaded. Framing a statement of defence is not the only ground for ordering particulars,

especially very basic particulars.

*B.Practicalities
Particulars of a plea of a collateral contract are proper, and getting times, places, people and

Particulars are required to allow a proper defence, but a demand for particulars should not

TheProceedings Against the Crown Actof Alberta specifically allows for a demand for particulars, and that is often an appropriate step, but this Act does not call for more disclosure It is rarely worth resisting your opponent's request for further and better particulars.Normally any expense and inconvenience of giving further particulars is much less than the expense and delay of resisting them and fighting a chambers application.Usually your opponent will be content with the particulars which you supply, so long as they really do attempt to answer the questions

which he or she has asked.Resisting particulars makes you look untrustworthy or unreasonable, so an application for particulars is likely to succeed unless it is unreasonable at first blush.His application is certainly likely to succeed if R.13.6 or 13.7 has been violated.If your client cannot give the particulars, that is often a sign that you do not know whether you have grounds for suit (or defence), and you and your client are just bluffing or gambling. be a delaying tactic, nor a substitute for what can be obtained through record production and

questioning. In a complex case, the case management judge must be left some elbow room to keep the suit moving and not bog down in interlocutory matters.Only in a clear case will the Court of Appeal interfere.So orders for particulars bythe plaintiffs may be upheld, though the defendants filed no affidavit or the statement of defence had already been filed.

*C.Hollow Excuses It is fallacious to suggest that a respondent or defendant does not need particulars of allegations about that respondent's (or defendant's) own conduct. It is no bar to ordering further particulars, that the party seeking them already knows "the facts". The defendant may know the

facts, but that is not the question.He or she does not know what the plaintiff alleges or will allege. The defendant may be innocent. *D. Procedure Under R.3.61(1), demand must be made before moving for an order to compel more particulars. The demand has a style of cause and an exact recital of what particulars are desired, such as

"the time and date and place of the conversation alleged in paragraph 4 of the statement of claim."

*(b)served on each of the other parties within 10 days after the date that*the amended pleading referred to in subrule (3) is served.*(5) If a party has pleaded in response to a pleading that is subsequently amended*and served on that party and the party does not file and serve a further response*to the amended pleading,the party is assumed to rely on the party's unamended*pleading in response to the amended pleading referred to in subrule (3).

Rule 3.62 lets a party unilaterally amend his or her pleading without anyone else's permission or agreement.It allows that in two circumstances: 1.where the pleadings have not yet closed;in other words, before the deadline for the other

side to file a later pleading answering it: R.3.62(1)(a); 2.within a few days after the opposing side has amended its pleading:R.3.62(3). It is not clear whether, or how much, the new unilateral amendment must be related to the opponent's amendment.

Rule 3.62 allows changes to the parties. Rule 3.62 will not apply after an action is certified as a class action. If the suit is begun by Originating Application (e.g.for judicial review), no Rule closes the

pleadings, and they close when the application is first heard by a judge (even if the judge makes a decision on the merits. Because of R.3.62(3), the court is more receptive to an amendment to the statement of claim necessitated by a plea in the statement of defence, even if the pleadings have been closed for a few months.

The Clerk will often not know when these times expire, and may have to assume that they have not expired, and accept the proffered amendment.Rule 3.62 lets one add a new cause of action closely related to the existing one, especially when it flows from facts in the original statement of claim.But court permission is necessary if some defendants have an arguable limitations defence.
