*B.General*1.Usual Rule Though new R.3.65(1) does not carry forward 1968 R.132's reference to determining the real question in issue, R.1.2(2)(a) does so for all the Rules. The 2010 Rules have not changed the general principles of amending pleadings.

An application formally to amend is needed.Pleadings do matter. The law favors amendments to pleadings, to help identify the real issues, prevent multiplicity of pleadings, and to keep suits moving promptly, which movement is in the public interest.

The accepted general principle is that any pleading can be amended at any time, no matter how careless is the party seeking to amend. There were four firm exceptions to that general rule 2Hur v. 726913 Alta.2013 ABQB 208, [2013] AR Uned 259 (¶ 29, cf.id.at ¶ 56).

*2.Possible Obstacles to Amendment These are procedural.More basic bars to amendment are in Parts C to F below. A legal bar to the cause of action motivating the proposed amendment may not be a fatal obstacle to amending, if the new allegationsmay be relevant to other issues in the suit. And an

expired limitation period does not make the original statement of claim a nullity, so it can still be amended;error does not necessarily produce nullity. The court has a discretion whether the plaintiff can amend the statement of claim to add further claims of damages after the defendant is noted in default but before damages are assessed. Whether to allow an amendment to a statement of claim is discretionary, and on appeal the standard of review is reasonableness, which involves a consideration of whether the relevant factors were given the proper weight, as well as whether there was an error in law. If no issue of a hopeless plea disclosing no cause of action is raised, then a court decision to permit or deny an amendment is discretionary, attracting deference.

What is"bad faith"barring an amendment to pleadings is not much discussed by case law. Bad faith barring amendments means intent (not necessarily the sole or primary intent, but a significant part of the intent) to inflict financial or emotional harm on the opponent, to conceal

amendment was allowed on appeal where no one could suggest any other evidence which could have been

[Footnote 13 continued from prior page]

relevant information, or to deceive another party or the court.Misguided but genuine intent to achieve the ostensible goal of the activity, without more bars a finding of bad faith.This is not a way to punish negligent or careless delay."Bad faith"does not open up a broad inquiry into past litigation conduct, which would be disproportionate and expensive . Waiting and not applying to amend, until later applying in order to stall a pending summary

evidence, can be bad faith.

Contrary evidence is no bar. But some evidence in favor of the amendment alone is not necessarily enough, and the evidence can be weighed to a degree. Amending the prayer for relief (on how to calculate damages) is less important than amendments to the body of the pleading, and an expert report filed for trial is enough to support it.

Where the evidence and the pleadings are both uncertain, the court cannot make an intelligent decision.The matter must be sent back to hear the amendment motion, based on relevant evidence for that motion.

*I.Retroactivity An amendment replaces the original version of the pleading, which ceases to have any effect? Usually an amendment is presumed to have effect retroactively to the date that the pleading now amended was first filed.An amendment of the statement of claim takes effect from commencement of the suit.That is why (apart from statute) limitation periods are usually a bar to

amendments adding a cause of action or a party, and why an amendment to a statement ofclaim cannot rely on events or causes of action arising only after the action was begun. One can, however, amend adefenceto reflect events during the suit. However, amendments are sometimes allowed on conditions, and one condition may be that

the amendment not be retroactive.That is sometimes a way to handle a dispute as to whether the limitation period had expired by the time the application to amend was filed.Similarly, an amendment may be allowed subject to any limitations defences accrued by then.

Rule 3.65(3) has much less significance than appears on the surface.The court can always Furthermore, that subrule is likely confined to a case where the court merely permits a party to make an amendment, maybe even just an unspecified amendment.Alberta orders usually direct a precise amendment, thus going beyond mere permission to a party, so R.3.65(3) may not apply to such an order. time makes the amendment inoperative,sed quaere. *4.Marking Amendments colored type;modern color printers and photocopiers may change that.(If one is underlining but using a black-and-white printer or photocopier, it is best to underline changes with light pencil lines over the faint black lines on the copies.) The traditional colours are red for the first amendment, green for the second, violet for the

third, and yellow for the fourth.But where a judge ordered many amendments to a statement of claim (in order to avoid striking it out), he dispensed with colored underlining, as it would merely confuse. A judge mildly criticized complex oft-amended pleadings as hard to read, especially as the proposed Amended Amended statement of claim was not properly underlined.

for contempt);13.13 (requirements for all documents);13.19 (requirements for affidavits); 14.74 (summary disposition of appeals) *A.Aims and Practicalities*1.New Rules The 2010 Rules of Court have not changed the test set by previous case law for striking out.

On the test, see further Part D.2 below. However, the courts have adopted a new"culture shift" for summary judgment (on which see R.7.3n).So it has been suggested that similarly, courts should become more willing to strike out pleadings, though the test is still the same:a reasonable prospect of the pleading (and the case alleged in it) succeeding. This new culture is not entirely irrelevant, but one must be careful.

Summary judgment is a decision on evidence;striking out is about unilateral unsworn claims, ignoring all possible evidence. *2.Drawbacks More time and money is wasted over this Rule than any other. 3.68(2). Sometimes a motion to strike out a statement of claim is based on its being frivolous, vexatious, embarrassing or an abuse of process, rather than on absence of cause of action. Frivolity refers to a plea so palpably bad that no real argument is needed to show how bad it is.

It is an abuse of process not to challenge in court a logging permit (on grounds of failure to consult an Indian Band), commit a trespass by blocking a railway line, and then to plead the failure to consult. That is probably a collateral attack and so forbidden.

See Part C.3 below. *7.Who Applies andWhen A defendant who has not filed a statement of defence can move to strike out the statement of claim as disclosing no cause of action or as being vexatious. And a recent decision holds that the filing of a statement of defence is not an immutable bar to later moving to strike the statement of

claim for want of a cause of action. Rule 3.68 applies to statements of claim just as much as to other pleadings. If the defendant is slow in moving to strike out the statement of claim, he may not get costs even if he wins. Generally, one should attack one's opponent's pleadings all at once, not in

instalments. A previousex parteorder giving leave to serve the statement of claimex jurisis no bar to a motion to strike it out under R.3.68(2)(b).

and the Rule's prohibition on adducing evidence on such a motion militates against any such notion, especially as the statement of claim is interpreted generously on the motion to strike it, Where the real issue was the constitutionality of legislation clearly purporting to bar a suit of this sort, but the plaintiff had had lots of chances in the past to attack its constitutionality, with

Once parts of a pleading are struck out, even reasonable amendments to cure the problem

and as facts pled are usually assumed to be true. better procedures, it was too late to move to strike the statement of claim based on a constitutional issue.

Because there are big differences between striking out and summary judgment, especially whether evidence is allowed, it is unhelpful to blend requests for both in one written application. That mixture tends to allow inadmissible evidence and to color assessing the pleadings, which is wrong. Not all paragraphs in a statement of claim will be freestanding claims, and they may usefully

give background, context, or narrative, relevant enough to other parts of the pleading not struck out, so those paragraphs should not be struck out;the plaintiff needs some leeway in how to tell his or her story. If one defendant makes a good case to strike out part of the statement of claim, it can be struck out against all defendants, even one with no counsel who (as an executor) cannot speak

for himself. Pleadings should be read generously, accommodating drafting deficiencies. Full indemnity costs may be given on striking out and making vexatious litigation orders.

On a statement of claim issued by a company which had been struck off the register, but later restored, see theKhuranacase. A defendant counterclaimed, seeking damages on the ground that the main action itself was an abuse of process and so atort.If the facts pled in the counterclaim were true, the tort might be made out.So it should not be struck out.

Rule 3.68 is not the tool to use when a litigant denies that she authorized the filing (by a non- lawyer) of some pleading purporting to be by or for her.

*R.3.68(4)PART 3:COURT ACTIONS Nor does commencing one vexatious proceeding necessarily make that plaintiff a vexatious litigant;for example the hopeless suit could be the product of ignorance. Still less is one instance of abuse of process the same as vexatiousness.

(b) Tests The tests for declaring someone to be a vexatious litigant and barring suits by him or her are discussed. Declaring apersona vexatious litigant is extraordinary and to be used only when other procedural orders or sanctions or costs, have been ineffective, and a pattern of repeated and persistent abuse is necessary.

Some earmarks are also discussed. Those include hopeless proceedings, failure to obey court orders and directions, unfounded allegations of dishonesty, frequent unsuccessful appeals, and multiple suits. Or persistent attempts (sometimes by collateral attacks) to relitigate matters already decided. Or Or persistently starting new lawsuits to bypass litigation management.

What records and evidence can be relied on to find a litigant vexatious, is discussed. In making a vexatious litigant order, the judge may refer to the public record to see other litigation by the same litigant against other people.For example, court decisions from other provinces about the same litigant, found on Canlii.The court can take judicial notice of court records. Past abusive

*habeas corpusmotions, with bad motive or strong unfounded accusations, are especially bad. The court then is not confined to looking at previous litigation in courts;it can also look at litigation before administrative tribunals. If a litigant is accused of being abusive, evidence of his or her intent, motivation, ideology, and

behavioral pathology, can be highly relevant. On an application by a person declared previously to be vexatious, for permission to bring a new suit, full evidence is necessary;he or she must put his or her best foot forward. (f)Later Permission to Appeal or Sue

Orders often forbid a vexatious litigant to start new lawsuits without court permission.The tests for giving the vexatious litigant leave to sue or appealaftersuch an order are discussed. A list of seven reasons to reject such a request for permission to start or continue a court proceeding, are found in some recent decisions. It is very relevant that the proposed new suit or appeal duplicates

old litigation and makes allegations which could not give a cause of action. Good evidence that the proposed new proceeding would have merit, is important. So is whether it would duplicate existing or past litigation. And the evidence should explain the circumstances of suing afresh.

(g) Notice The Court of Appeal has cut down the scope of applications outside the legislation (Judicature Act), and that has two implications for notice.

(a)The person against whom relief or a finding of vexatiousness is sought must get notice and a chance to defend, unless in special cases the legal principles for dispensing with notice of an application (i.e.forex parteapplications) are met, and then such an order should be temporary.

(b)The applicant must give notice of his or her application to the Attorney General of Alberta. (h) Costs If a litigant succeeds in having the court end the lawsuit (via Civil Practice Note 7), usually that litigant get costs, and did here where the suits sought a million dollars.A vexatious litigant

may have to pay costs to the court, especially if disregarding warnings. And for repeat offences, increased costs. *3.Appeals An order prohibiting launching more legal proceedings about a matrimonial dispute, without

court leave, did not bar appeals to the Court of Appeal. Leave to appeal to the Court of Appeal from a vexatious-litigant order or its implementation, often requires leave to appeal. The standard of review on appeal from a motion to strike out is not totally clear. Whetherto

strike out a pleading under R.3.68(2)(d) is a question of mixed law and fact, to be reviewed on a palpable and overriding error standard. Whether to declare someone a vexatious litigant is a discretionary decision reviewed on an appellate standard of reasonableness. On appeals from

such orders, see the commentary on R.14.5(4).A vexatious litigant seeking leave to appeal a decision must show some obvious error of law in that decision, or an issue of sufficient importance to justify a further appeal.

If a litigant is very persistent in relitigating the same topics and ingenious in finding new people to sue over the same topics, narrower orders or restrictions to be administered by court clerks will not work, so leave to file anything in the Court of King's Bench may be barred unless the Chief Justice or the Associate Chief Justice gives leave. motions may well be dismissed. of his or her communications, and orders that they be sent to a special email address. On the

procedure, and the two steps, see theHokcase. Whether to find asuitvexatious, and whether to find alitigantvexatious, are not the same thing, and if vexatiousness is shown only with respect to one opponent, than an order requiring

Sometimes a vexatious litigant is so persistent or abusive to staff, that the court bars him from attending the office of the Clerk or Registrar, or even the court house. Or restricts the types leave to sue should be limited to suits against the same opponent or persons associated with him. And if asuitis only partly vexatious, then the remainder of the statement of claim should

not be struck out. The courts have inherent power to protect subordinate entities, such as a statutory professional disciplinary body, from vexatious litigants.A repeat complainant was prohibited from starting complaints under the Engineering legislation without leave of the Court of King's Bench etc., and

forbidden to apply under any other name. Though a superior court can assist a lower court or a statutory tribunal, it should not be quick to bar a persistent litigant from contacting or communicating with or applying to that body, even if that litigant may make repetitive or unfounded accusations or complaints.Some such bodies have power to protect themselves (e.g.by passing Rules or giving certain officers the power to screen, or the exclusive power to receive communications).

Some do not want to screen or want help.The tribunal should ask for or agree to such help, and all applications involving such a tribunal require notice to that tribunal. And any such assistance should be targetted and shaped to the past and likely future misconduct and the facts of the particular case.

The court has inherent jurisdiction, and a positive duty, to ensure that clearly highly unsuitable people (especially non-lawyers) do not represent litigants. conditions inFreeman v. Cdn.Nat.Res. If a vexatious litigant simply flouts an order requiring permission to sue again, the new suit will be struck out, and the existing restricted-access order may be tightened. There are many recent examples of that, too many to note here. After previous warnings and threats of fines, a vexatious litigant who disobeyed previous court

orders and filed forbidden documents, was fined $1000 under R.10.49(1). The Court of Queen's Bench had made two blanket Master Orders prohibiting Clerks from accepting for filing papers having certain distinctive characteristics of "organized pseudolegal commercial arguments", such as certain very odd formats for names, or names said to be copyright or trademarked, or with fingerprints, blood, or postage stamps attached.

*3.Factual Element consistent with the facts pleaded by the statement of claim, which might give a cause of action, the claim cannot be struck out. For example, a statement of claim may not suggest a familiar cause of action, but if the facts pled could (depending on the details) show a duty of care by

of fiduciary duties. The law of negligence in Canada is still somewhat fluid and being rebuilt, especially respecting who owes a duty of care to whom, and negligence suits by someone closely involved are particularly unsuitable for striking out summarily before trial, as are many questions of remoteness of damage. *4.Miscellaneous*F.Res Judicata (a) Introduction This is an important doctrine. Relitigating the same question between the same parties is a great evil, Thisdoctrine and fairly common at present.So is another curse, fragmented litigation.

is designed to bar those evils. Even if a plea discloses a cause of action or defence and is otherwise proper, it may be struck out if it challenges or ignores previous valid legal proceedings.

(b)Topic Not Raised Before

Of course often there is no bar to relitigating a topic not in issue before, and the fact that it could have been raised before is not the only or a sufficient question;the test is also whether it *shouldhave been raised before. See further subpart 2 below.

up later with a new legal argument is not enough to attack the first decision on a new ground.

that interest may not be claimed on passing accounts does not mean (in a new proper suit) that There are various branches ofres judicata. For a discussion of issue estoppel and cause

of action estoppel, see the Civil Procedure Encyclopedia. The various branches differ somewhat in whether they are based on the interests of the litigants, or upon those of the justice system.

(c)Reversed on appeal

A ruling at the first trial is notres judicataif an appeal court orders a new trial in an unrestricted

manner? Or otherwise reverses the earlier decision. (d)Family law? There is an extended discussion of when a decision on support in a family law matter isres *judicata, and how that squares with power to vary such orders. There may not beres judicata

in child welfare matters? But a consent order for division of matrimonial property does workres *judicata(until it is set aside). (e)Perjury or Fraud Perjury in an exception tores judicata, but the Crown cannot claim that exception unless it

tenders additional evidence to that available with reasonable diligence at the time of the first trial. The exception tores judicatafor fraud is discussed. On new evidence obtainable the first time with due diligence, see theBurcevskicase.

The exception tores judicatafor new evidence is narrow and requires a high degree of proof. The new evidence must be practically conclusive.It must entirely change the entire case, not just some ingredient or aspect of the case, and not merely be an additional ingredient.And it must not have been obtainable before the due diligence.England and some provinces require fraud or unconscionable conduct.The Alberta Court of Appeal does not go that far.

events. *6.Functus Officio Generally speaking, once a formal order is entered, the Court isfunctus officio, and cannot correct its order, even if it appears to be wrong. But entering a formal judgment in the face of an

application to vary the judgment, may not make the courtfunctuswhere the party entering has no such intent. exception. Even in a criminal case, a judge can announce the result (conviction) and say that reasons will follow, and then a couple of weeks later give detailed reasons.The doctrine offunctus officio does not apply.

*2.Court Records Activities inside and outside court, and inside and outside Alberta, can provide relevant evidence, and the court may take judicial notice of public records on this topic. One affidavit the judge declined to look at the evidence, as a motion to strike out should be based on the

pleadings alone. The court can look at the decision or pleadings in a closely-related suit to find a binding decision;that is not evidence? But if the statement of claim is to be struck out for want of jurisdiction, evidence is necessary, as not every fact pled will be assumed to be true.Court jurisdiction or not depends on the facts

surrounding dispute, not how the legal issues are framed. *3.InadmissibleTypes of Evidence Many parts of an affidavit were mere argument, not facts, and many were irrelevant and offended R.3.68(3)(a), so the whole affidavit was struck. An affidavit can be struck if it is

Information Note This rule and the following rules of this Division apply to all actions, whether started by statement of claim or by originating application.

Defined Terms

claim, Court, defendant, party Related Provisions 2.6 (representative actions);2.9 (class actions);12.9 (joining claim for divorce and matrimonial property);12.10 (joining claim for unjust enrichment);12.13 (joint claims for divorce);14.9 (joinder of appeals). *A.Introduction
There should not be as many separate lawsuits as there are parties or causes of action. Though the language is permissive, in most cases it is preferable to read "may"as "should," because it is usually to everyone's benefit to keep things together.Accordingly, defendants should be left in an action unless they can demonstrate that they should be treated separately.From a plaintiff's perspective, this will help avoid the pitfalls of inadvertent election, release or waiver. *B.Standing
*1.References Who has enough standing to sue or be a party is considered in Chapters 7 and 8 of the Civil Procedure Encyclopedia, and Rr.2.10 n.C, 3.68 n.D.4 and 3.69 n.B above. *2.Nature of Standing or Interest The basic rule is that a plaintiff needs some legitimate right or interest, and that an unaffected

busybody cannot sue. A person without standing cannot apply to the court or sue, e.g.for child guardianship. There is a considerable discussion of standing (private vs.public), in theReece No standing means this topic is not the plaintiff's business. case.

Public interest standing needs a serious justiciable issue. Usually whether a plaintiff has standing is best decided conclusively at the early stage, not after pleadings, discovery, evidence, experts, and a trial, all of which would be wasted if there were no standing.Here the judge was not asked to decide standing only on an interim basis, nor to decide merely that it was not obviously absent.It was proper to make the decision on standing

permanent. The laws of standing are purposive, and balance access to justice with conservation of judicial resources.Existence of a parallel claim is not conclusive, and if the question is alternative avenues of challenge, they must be practical, and dangers must be weighed. See also R.2.10 n.C.

**PAGE 221
PART 3:COURT ACTIONSR.3.69(3) 3-221

One should distinguish derivative, collective, and combined claims. If the suit is brought under theBusiness Corporations Act, the proposed plaintiff must be a"complainant"under that Act,

the directors must have got notice, the application for the suit must be in good faith, and the suit must be in the company's best interests. "Good faith"means merely not frivolous or vexatious, objectively or subjectively. "Best interests" involves likelihood of success whether the suit would be economical, whether it would alienate customers or harm the company's reputation. On a

case management. After an order to try two suits together, consolidation will not be ordered. When two suits were ordered tried together and had a common issue, the evidence in one could be considered in the other. Also, it should be possible to make more detailed orders later to reduce the duplication of

evidence. And limitations defences may need preservation through a condition in the consolidation order. A third alternative to consolidation, especially if a party is willing, is to try one or a few cases first as a test case, and then stay the rest pending the result. But if two cases are to be tried together, staying one is not proper.

(b)Arguability, Not Certainty

(c)Test:Who is Affected

There need only be an arguable cause of action against a proposed new defendant, not It is undesirable that two suits reach opposite conclusions about the same fact question, so even a non-party who would not be bound by a judgment, can become a party, where that non- party would be directly affected.

Rule 3.74 tends for the most part to be broadly construed, with detriment or prejudice employed as a test. A party should not be added for the mere purpose of being questioned (examined for discovery). Adding the Crown to a suit in which it is not interested is improper, especially if it has already intervened, and wishing it to pay costs is not a proper motive.

To add a party, the person so applying must show two things.First, that he or she has a legal interest. The second test is not completely plain, but it seems to be either justice and convenience, or lack of adequate protection if he or she is not a party.Rule 3.74(3) requires absence of prejudice as well.The 1968 Rule required that the party be "interested", but the

4Kopr v.Kopr2006 ABQB 405, 403 AR 29.

A party seeking to add new parties must give notice of the application to the existing parties, and cannot proceedex parte. All parties to a suit can be heard on all applications. Theperson proposed to be added as a party can participate in the application and oppose it. A defendant always has standing to challenge the status of the plaintiff to sue. A mere busybody plaintiff may be struck out.

Where a person has a status or interest and the common law or a statute clearly makes him or her someone who should be served, and no legislation permits dispensing with service, that that person is alleged dangerous (or proven dangerous) does not permit such dispensation.Instead the remedy is some redaction to remove private access information about where the parties suing, and their families, may be found or contacted.

When a party learns of a possible cause of action against a non-party, it cannot simply point that out and say that it is reserving the right to move later to add that non-party. Adding a

necessary defendant by amendment of the statement of claim without leave or consent is not a nullity, and the court may not strike it out? The Court of Appeal should not add a new party on its own initiative, even in a child custody case;that is unfair to other parties. If the topic of proper parties was not raised in the trial court or

Court of Appeal, it cannot later (after appellate judgment) be raised before one justice of appeal; but if the suit is ongoing, it could be raised in the Court of King's Bench. One may add a new or extra plaintiff partway through a suit where there is no indication that that would prejudice the defendants and the extra plaintiff appears to have an interest.

Whether there are other necessary parties not yet joined cannot be sorted out on appeal if evidence may be necessary;the case may have to be returned to the superior court to look into that. The court can add a party only on application by a party showing good reasons and absence of prejudice.

Adding a new defendant later is in effect suing him or her.In general, it is not possible to add a new defendant after the limitation period has expired. Nor then to add a new plaintiff, whether giving repose after a certain length of time almost as much as adding a new defendant. On an application to add a new defendant faced with an objection that the limitation period has passed,

the test is the same as if the new defendant had applied for summary judgment:reasonable doubt, based on the plaintiffs'version of the facts. In proceedings where extra parties or interveners are added and then the added parties want relief, one must watch for the statutory limitation period (which for judicial review quashing in a labour case is short).

Now limitation periods are doubly dangerous because booking motions or originating applications, and filing any kind of court document is now slow, complex, and uncertain.See long discussion of these problems in R.3.2n.B.4.

The grounds of the Supreme Court of Canada decision are first, compliance with a court order, which is a statutory exception to privacy. Second, they found implied consent by the mortgagor (debtor).The privacy legislation does not cut down courts'powers, nor interfere with the rules of Court about production.Another mortgagee was not a stranger to the court proceeding.

Defined Terms

claim, foreclosure action, party, plaintiff

Related Provisions

3.41 (no defence in foreclosure);6.5 (notice in foreclosure actions); Part 9, Div.5 (foreclosure rules);11.23 (address for service in foreclosure) Alberta (unlike some other provinces) postpones questions of subsequent encumbrancers in a foreclosure suit, and they are not ordinarily to be made parties. The subsequent encumbrancer avoids the limitation period by riding the first mortgagee's coattails in its foreclosure suit, and though

a limitation could then be pled by the mortgagor, the time likely would not have expired when the main statement of claim was issued, and time could have been extended by acknowledgment. For foreclosure and summary judgment, the limitation period may bar action on a mortgage by a deemed transferee of it who has paid it off.Part payment and acknowledgment of debt does not suspend time, if the person so acting is not the mortgagee then and could not enforce it then.

Ontario courts had held that general privacy legislation barred one mortgagee from giving a mortgage statement to another mortgagee of the same property, despite the latter's right to redeem that other mortgage!The Supreme Court of Canada reversed. The plaintiff mortgagee does not fully control a foreclosure suit, as every encumbrancer's

claim is also to be considered and adjudicated, and the court has discretion over the remedy which the mortgagee gets, and the court has certain leanings, especially if there is equity in the property, and there are special rules for when and how the mortgagee may buy the property.A writ-holder may be given control of the same or foreclosure proceeding. A mortgage covenant giving up possession is illegal, and so even after a closed, court-

approved sale, the mortgagee cannot change the locks.
